People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

At home I’ll start doing something and a little noise or someone moving around can pull me off, but the warehouse is where it’s really causing problems—like the beepers or forklift alarms make me lose my place counting, and then I end up putting in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do these concentration problems cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s not panic or anything, but it does bother me. I get frustrated and worried because at work one little sound can make me lose my place, and then I find out I entered the wrong inventory numbers. It makes me anxious that I’m going to keep making mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're having trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might misplace something here and there like anybody, but I haven’t noticed a big problem remembering things that just happened. It’s more that I get thrown off in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have been more keyed up, but it comes out more as doing stuff than just fidgeting. At work I’ve been taking on extra safety-improvement projects, and at home I’m signing up for more art workshops than I usually would. I’ve been making all these lists and schedules like I’m running some big operation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven by a motor, even when you're not necessarily doing anything physical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a good way to put it. After my shifts, I used to be tired and just sit down, but lately I come home kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage, because just sitting on the couch feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems at work or in your relationships because of these concentration and restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. At work I’ve made some inventory mistakes, enough that I had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of settling into his retirement together.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily irritated or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I can fix the whole house and straighten out everything at work before dinner. But then if my husband just questions one of my plans, I’ll snap at him over something small. It’s not really like me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more that I get thrown off in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have been more keyed up, but it comes out more as doing stuff than just fidgeting. At work I’ve been taking on extra safety-improvement projects, and at home I’m signing up for more art workshops than I usually would. I’ve been making all these lists and schedules like I’m running some big operation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven by a motor, even when you're not necessarily doing anything physical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a good way to put it. After my shifts, I used to be tired and just sit down, but lately I come home kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage, because just sitting on the couch feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems at work or in your relationships because of these concentration and restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. At work I’ve made some inventory mistakes, enough that I had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of settling into his retirement together.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily irritated or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I can fix the whole house and straighten out everything at work before dinner. But then if my husband just questions one of my plans, I’ll snap at him over something small. It’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt extremely anxious or worried for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it extreme anxiety, no. I do get worked up or tense sometimes, but I can usually tell it’s tied to something going on. I haven’t noticed big waves of worry coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these changes in your concentration and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I first noticed it about a month ago, around when my husband stopped working outside the home. Our routine changed pretty suddenly, and that’s when I started feeling more scattered and restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors other than your husband's retirement that might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nothing big comes to mind besides that change at home. Work is work, and life has the usual stuff, but I can’t think of any other major stressor that lines up with how I’ve been feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these concentration or restlessness issues as a child or teenager?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember having this as a kid or teenager. I mean, I’m sure I got restless sometimes like anyone, but nothing that stood out or felt like a real problem back then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been buying more than I should at craft markets—paints, frames, tools, things for projects I was sure I’d make useful. Then I see the credit card bill and I feel embarrassed, like, why did I spend that much?\n\nDoctor: Are your husband's retirement and your current state affecting your relationship with him in any significant way beyond the arguments you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. It’s not just little arguments anymore—at home it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of actually settling into this retirement thing together. And with me being off lately, making mistakes at work too, like inventory errors I had to double-check with my supervisor, I think that stress is coming home with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. At work I’ve made some inventory mistakes, enough that I had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of settling into his retirement together.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily irritated or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I can fix the whole house and straighten out everything at work before dinner. But then if my husband just questions one of my plans, I’ll snap at him over something small. It’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt extremely anxious or worried for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it extreme anxiety, no. I do get worked up or tense sometimes, but I can usually tell it’s tied to something going on. I haven’t noticed big waves of worry coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these changes in your concentration and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I first noticed it about a month ago, around when my husband stopped working outside the home. Our routine changed pretty suddenly, and that’s when I started feeling more scattered and restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors other than your husband's retirement that might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nothing big comes to mind besides that change at home. Work is work, and life has the usual stuff, but I can’t think of any other major stressor that lines up with how I’ve been feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these concentration or restlessness issues as a child or teenager?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember having this as a kid or teenager. I mean, I’m sure I got restless sometimes like anyone, but nothing that stood out or felt like a real problem back then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been buying more than I should at craft markets—paints, frames, tools, things for projects I was sure I’d make useful. Then I see the credit card bill and I feel embarrassed, like, why did I spend that much?\n\nDoctor: Are your husband's retirement and your current state affecting your relationship with him in any significant way beyond the arguments you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. It’s not just little arguments anymore—at home it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of actually settling into this retirement thing together. And with me being off lately, making mistakes at work too, like inventory errors I had to double-check with my supervisor, I think that stress is coming home with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period in your life where you didn't have these concentration and restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before about a month ago, I don’t remember this being an issue. It really started after my husband stopped working outside the home and our usual routine changed. Since then I’ve felt more scattered and restless.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’ve been making all these lists and schedules like I’m running some big operation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven by a motor, even when you're not necessarily doing anything physical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a good way to put it. After my shifts, I used to be tired and just sit down, but lately I come home kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting bins in the garage, because just sitting on the couch feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems at work or in your relationships because of these concentration and restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. At work I’ve made some inventory mistakes, enough that I had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of settling into his retirement together.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily irritated or upset by things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I can fix the whole house and straighten out everything at work before dinner. But then if my husband just questions one of my plans, I’ll snap at him over something small. It’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt extremely anxious or worried for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it extreme anxiety, no. I do get worked up or tense sometimes, but I can usually tell it’s tied to something going on. I haven’t noticed big waves of worry coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these changes in your concentration and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I first noticed it about a month ago, around when my husband stopped working outside the home. Our routine changed pretty suddenly, and that’s when I started feeling more scattered and restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors other than your husband's retirement that might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nothing big comes to mind besides that change at home. Work is work, and life has the usual stuff, but I can’t think of any other major stressor that lines up with how I’ve been feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these concentration or restlessness issues as a child or teenager?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember having this as a kid or teenager. I mean, I’m sure I got restless sometimes like anyone, but nothing that stood out or felt like a real problem back then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been buying more than I should at craft markets—paints, frames, tools, things for projects I was sure I’d make useful. Then I see the credit card bill and I feel embarrassed, like, why did I spend that much?\n\nDoctor: Are your husband's retirement and your current state affecting your relationship with him in any significant way beyond the arguments you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. It’s not just little arguments anymore—at home it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of actually settling into this retirement thing together. And with me being off lately, making mistakes at work too, like inventory errors I had to double-check with my supervisor, I think that stress is coming home with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period in your life where you didn't have these concentration and restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before about a month ago, I don’t remember this being an issue. It really started after my husband stopped working outside the home and our usual routine changed. Since then I’ve felt more scattered and restless.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your husband's retirement and the changes it brought to your life are the main reason you're feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. It’s been about a month, pretty much since he stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. That’s when I started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if your husband went back to work or if you found a way to adjust to his retirement?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, but I think if we adjusted to the new routine, it might help. This has really only been going on about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our usual routine changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention and restlessness consistently across different settings, such as both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been in both places. At work I’ve made some inventory mistakes and had to go back and double-check with my supervisor. And at home, it’s been showing up too—my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of settling into him being retired.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention and restlessness for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. It’s really been about a month, since my husband stopped working outside the home and our regular routine changed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },
